#10870. Why Transactional Emails Fail After Deployment: A Practical Debugging Guide for Developers

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Transactional emails are easy to overlook during application development. A registration form works, the database stores the user's information, and the backend successfully triggers an email. Everything appears to be functioning as expected.

Then a user reports that their password-reset message never arrived.

The problem might not be in the email template or the sending function. It could involve DNS configuration, authentication failures, message formatting, queue processing, recipient-server policies, or an incorrectly configured production environment.

For developers building SaaS applications, ecommerce platforms, and web services, reliable email delivery is an important part of the user experience. An account-verification message that never arrives can prevent someone from accessing an application, while a delayed security notification can create unnecessary confusion.

Understanding how to investigate these problems requires looking beyond the email-sending function. This guide explores a practical debugging workflow, the technical layers involved, and the tools developers can use to identify common email delivery failures.

One of the first mistakes developers make when troubleshooting email is assuming that a successful API response means the message has reached the recipient.

When an application submits an email through an SMTP server or an email service provider's API, the provider may accept the request and return a successful response. However, the receiving mail server still needs to process the message.

Depending on its configuration and policies, the receiving server might:

Accept the message and place it in the inbox.

Accept the message but classify it as spam.

Reject the message because of authentication or policy issues.

Accept the message but place it in another folder.

A successful request therefore confirms only that a particular stage of the process completed.

Developers should distinguish between three important states:

Submission: The application successfully handed the message to its sending provider.

Acceptance: The receiving mail server accepted responsibility for the message.

Inbox placement: The message became visible in the recipient's expected inbox.

These distinctions make troubleshooting considerably easier. Instead of immediately rewriting an email template, developers can identify which stage is actually failing.

Before investigating external email infrastructure, examine the application's own implementation.

A common example is a password-reset workflow. The application receives a request, generates a temporary token, creates a reset URL, and sends an email containing that URL.

Several problems can occur before the message even reaches an email provider.

The application generates an expired token.

The reset URL points to a staging environment.

An asynchronous task fails before processing the message.

The email service credentials are incorrect.

An API request times out without being retried.

The application records success before confirming the provider's response.

Start by logging the important stages of the process.

For example, a Node.js application might use structured logging:

This example assumes that the email provider returns a message identifier. Adapt the response fields to the actual SDK being used.

Notice that the logs identify the recipient through an internal ID rather than exposing their complete email address.

Avoid logging passwords, authentication tokens, reset URLs, or other sensitive information.

The objective is to establish a traceable record of what happened without introducing additional security risks.

Email authentication is another important area to investigate when messages are rejected or consistently classified as spam.

Three commonly used mechanisms help receiving servers evaluate whether a message is authorized to represent a particular domain.

Sender Policy Framework (SPF) allows a domain owner to specify which servers are authorized to send messages using that domain.

This information is published through DNS.

A simplified SPF record might look like this:

The actual record must reflect the sending services authorized by the domain owner.

Forgetting to authorize a new email provider.

Publishing multiple SPF records for the same domain.

Leaving outdated sending services in the record.

Remember that SPF evaluates the sending infrastructure against the envelope sender domain. It does not automatically guarantee that the visible From address is aligned.

DomainKeys Identified Mail (DKIM) adds a cryptographic signature to outgoing messages.

The receiving server can retrieve the corresponding public key through DNS and verify that the signature is valid.

A mismatch between the configured selector and published record.

Developers integrating multiple email providers should verify that each provider has the correct signing configuration.

Domain-based Message Authentication, Reporting, and Conformance (DMARC) builds on SPF and DKIM.

It allows domain owners to define how receiving servers should handle messages that fail authentication and alignment requirements.

A basic monitoring-oriented DMARC record might look like this:

This is an illustrative configuration. The reporting address must be valid, and the domain should have an appropriate monitoring and policy-enforcement plan.

Developers can use MXToolbox to inspect DNS records and investigate common authentication problems.

For additional technical background, SitePoint's guide to HTML email development explains why email environments require different implementation considerations from ordinary web pages.

An email template that looks perfect in a browser can behave differently when rendered by Gmail, Outlook, Apple Mail, or another email client.

This happens because email clients do not implement HTML and CSS in exactly the same way.

A layout may break because of unsupported CSS properties, image restrictions, unexpected font rendering, or differences in how tables are handled.

For this reason, email testing should be part of the development process rather than something performed only after users report a problem.

HTML validation: Check the markup for malformed elements, missing attributes, and unnecessary code.

Rendering tests: Preview the email across desktop and mobile email clients.

Link verification: Confirm that buttons, links, and personalized URLs point to the correct destinations.

Image testing: Check whether the message remains understandable when images are blocked.

Content inspection: Review subject lines, sender information, and the plain-text alternative.

Deliverability checks: Examine authentication, content-related warnings, and potential inbox placement problems.

Developers can also incorporate pre-send diagnostics into their release workflow to identify potential deliverability problems before a message is sent to a larger audience.

The important point is to treat these checks as one part of quality assurance, not as a guarantee that every message will reach the primary inbox.

For more information about email rendering differences, SitePoint's article on essential HTML email coding tips provides useful examples of client-specific behavior.

Testing transactional emails with real customer addresses during development can create unnecessary problems.

A better approach is to use a dedicated testing environment that captures outgoing messages without delivering them to actual recipients.

For example, Mailpit provides a local SMTP server and web interface for inspecting messages during development.

The final output generated by the application.

Another option is Mailtrap, which provides email testing capabilities for development workflows.

Consider a registration feature that sends a welcome message whenever a new user creates an account.

Instead of sending hundreds of test messages to real addresses, developers can configure the development environment to capture them in a testing inbox.

This makes it possible to repeatedly test different templates without affecting actual customers or generating unnecessary email traffic.

A separate testing environment also reduces the risk of accidentally sending development notifications to production users.

Applications that send large numbers of emails often use background workers instead of processing every message during the original HTTP request.

This approach helps keep the application responsive, but it introduces another potential source of failures.

Imagine an ecommerce application that sends order confirmations after checkout.

The application creates an order successfully and places an email task in a queue. However, the background worker becomes unavailable before processing the task.

The customer receives an order confirmation much later than expected, or perhaps not at all.

To investigate these problems, developers should monitor:

Retries are particularly important, but they need to be implemented carefully.

A temporary network failure may justify another attempt. A permanent rejection caused by an invalid recipient address usually does not.

Exponential backoff can help prevent repeated requests from overwhelming an unavailable service.

In a production system, add random jitter to avoid having multiple workers retry simultaneously.

Also consider idempotency. If a worker processes the same task twice, the application should avoid sending duplicate transactional messages whenever possible.

A reliable queue architecture should distinguish between temporary failures and permanent failures rather than retrying every error indefinitely.

Testing before deployment is important, but it cannot reveal every issue that may occur in production.

Real email traffic introduces changing conditions, including recipient-server behavior, sender reputation, invalid addresses, and differences between email providers.

Developers should therefore establish ongoing monitoring.

For Gmail-related monitoring, Google Postmaster Tools provides information about domain reputation, spam rates, and authentication.

It is particularly useful for teams sending enough email to generate meaningful reporting data.

Another useful resource is Can I Email, which documents HTML and CSS feature support across email clients.

These tools address different problems. Monitoring sender reputation will not fix a broken password-reset URL, just as validating HTML will not resolve a DNS authentication failure.

The best approach is to monitor each layer independently.

A repeatable checklist helps developers investigate problems without overlooking important details.

Before releasing a new email feature, review the following:

Confirm that the application successfully submits the message.

Verify that provider credentials and environment variables are correct.

Inspect SPF, DKIM, and DMARC configuration.

Validate the HTML and plain-text versions.

Preview the message on desktop and mobile email clients.

Verify that development and production configurations are separated.

Check delivery events and provider error reports.

Monitor bounce and complaint rates after deployment.

Not every application needs an expensive testing platform or a complicated monitoring stack.

A small project might only require a local email testing environment, basic DNS checks, and structured application logs.

A larger SaaS platform may benefit from automated integration tests, provider webhooks, queue monitoring, authentication reports, and dedicated deliverability testing.

The right setup depends on the application's sending volume, complexity, and the importance of its email workflows.

Reliable email delivery requires more than calling an SMTP function or integrating an email API.

Developers need to understand the complete process, from application logic and message construction to authentication, queue processing, recipient-server acceptance, and inbox placement.

The most effective troubleshooting strategy is to isolate these layers instead of treating every missing message as a template problem.

Start with application logs, inspect provider responses, validate authentication records, test email rendering, and monitor production behavior.

By incorporating these checks into development and deployment workflows, teams can reduce avoidable failures and build applications that communicate more reliably with their users.

#10865. What Actually Happens When a Kafka Region Fails?

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Most Kafka deployments in the industry plan to survive the loss of a single Kafka broker or an availability zone. But that's not enough for critical Tier-0 infrastructure where downtime or data loss of even a few seconds could have a massive business impact.

In such scenarios, the failure model should consider not just the loss of a broker or a zone, but the loss of an entire region. The business is expected to operate without interruption even when a whole region goes down, and it should be possible to also roll the cluster safely while operating in that state, to apply any break-glass changes.

But what actually happens inside Kafka when an entire region goes down? What happens to the replicas, producer writes, and KRaft quorum? And what happens when that region comes back?

Let's walk through these scenarios using a Tier-0 multi-region Kafka architecture.

For a Tier-0 multi-region Kafka cluster, we need three different regions with isolated failure domains and a minimum of two brokers in each region.

Brokers in each region should be configured with rack awareness to indicate their failure domains as follows:

For Tier-0 workloads, each topic's partitions should be configured to have a replication factor (RF) of 6. Kafka's rack-aware replica assignment will spread the replicas across the three regions. If the automatic replica assignment doesn't allocate two replicas per region, we have to explicitly reassign replicas immediately after topic creation to meet our availability guarantees.

This way, if one region goes down entirely, we still have four replicas surviving and the cluster would still continue to operate.

But just running brokers in multiple regions is not the solution here. In fact, that's the easy part. The hard question is, what guarantee remains when one of those regions disappears, and does it solve the business needs?

The two brokers in that region become unavailable, leaving four surviving replicas across Regions B and C.

For any partitions whose leaders were in Region A, Kafka needs to elect new leaders from the surviving in-sync replicas.

But having four surviving replicas alone does not determine whether Kafka will continue accepting writes.

The next step is to define what is considered a successful write in this Kafka setup. For this, we need two configs:

min.insync.replicas determines the minimum number of replicas that should be present in the in-sync replica set (ISR) of a partition for Kafka to accept writes when producers use acks=all.

acks=all is a producer-side config that ensures that the producer will get an acknowledgment only when the write has been successfully replicated to the required in-sync replicas.

These two settings work together to satisfy the durability guarantees we want.

When all three regions are healthy, ISR would include all six replicas. Let's say one region goes down, then the ISR would drop down to four.

Since minISR is three, writes will still continue to work.

There may be temporary interruptions while Kafka detects the failure, elects new leaders, and clients reconnect to them. But once the surviving leaders are available, the cluster can continue accepting writes as long as the ISR requirements are met.

The goal isn't availability at any cost, but rather the system should be available as long as the durability guarantees that we designed for can be maintained.

Now let's make the scenario more interesting.

Region A is still down. We have four replicas surviving across Regions B and C.

What if we need to roll the cluster one broker at a time, to apply any break-glass changes?

During that time, the ISR can go down from four to three. But since minISR is three, writes will still continue to work.

Let's look at how the cluster behaves under different failure scenarios.

Kafka will stop accepting writes only when the surviving ISR drops below three.

This is an important distinction for Tier-0 workloads.

It's not sufficient to design a cluster that survives a region failure. We also need to consider whether the cluster can continue operating safely while already in that degraded state.

So far, we focused on the data plane. Now switching gears, the KRaft controllers have a completely different availability model.

They form the metadata quorum, and a majority is needed to continue operation.

For our setup, five controllers could be distributed as 2 + 2 + 1 across the three regions, requiring a majority of three to maintain quorum.

If either of the regions containing two controllers goes down, three controllers remain. If the region containing one controller goes down, four remain.

So, the controller quorum survives the loss of any one region.

But, unlike brokers, we cannot necessarily roll the controllers at that point because taking one controller down could cause the quorum to drop below three.

With a static quorum, one way to solve this is to use more controllers, and a 3 + 3 + 3 distribution would provide this. Losing one region would leave six controllers with room to still roll the controllers, as we only need five to maintain quorum.

On the flip side, an increase in quorum size also increases the coordination overhead. The official Kafka documentation typically recommends three or five controllers, which tolerate one or two concurrent failures respectively.

Alternatively, with dynamic KRaft quorum (KIP-853), introduced in Kafka 3.9, we can add or remove controllers from the voting quorum without restarting the cluster.

For example, with our 2 + 2 + 1 setup, if a region containing two controllers goes down, the remaining three controllers can still maintain quorum. We can then remove the failed controllers from the voting membership, one at a time, reducing the quorum size to three. This allows us to roll one of the surviving controllers while still maintaining the new majority of two.

However, dynamic quorum reconfiguration is not automatic. The surviving controllers must be able to maintain quorum and successfully commit the membership changes before we can proceed with the controller restart.

You need to consider whether you want the same availability guarantee for the controllers as that of the data plane, and whether dynamic quorum reconfiguration during a regional outage is acceptable for your Tier-0 workloads.

So far, we assumed that a region either works or goes down completely.

But the WAN itself becomes a failure mode.

In this setup, a partly working WAN between two regions can be more dangerous than a link that's completely down.

Increased latency, packet loss, or intermittent connectivity issues between regions can cause replication to lag, ultimately resulting in replicas falling out of ISR.

The three regions might be independently healthy, but if the connectivity between them is not, then the availability of the cluster might be impacted.

Going back to the CAP theorem, to tolerate a network partition, we must choose between consistency and availability.

So, if a partition's ISR falls below the configured min.insync.replicas of three due to a network partition, Kafka stops accepting writes with acks=all rather than weakening those guarantees.

Similarly, if the KRaft controller quorum is lost, then no metadata changes, like leader elections, can happen in the cluster, even though existing data-plane traffic may continue for some time.

The availability guarantees we discussed also come with their own costs.

The first cost is client latency. With cross-region replication and acks=all, the network latency between these regions directly affects producer latency. Consumers can also pay the WAN latency cost when consuming from partition leaders located in a different region.

The second cost is replication overhead. With RF=6, there is additional storage, network traffic, and replication-related coordination cost in the brokers compared to a smaller number of replicas.

So, in an architecture like this, monitoring the health of the network paths between these regions is as critical as monitoring the Kafka brokers and controllers.

Metrics like round-trip latency (RTT) between regions, packet loss, TCP retransmissions, connection errors/timeouts, and network throughput/bandwidth utilization are a good start.

When one region goes down, all the partition leaders in that region would move to the two other surviving regions.

This implies that those two regions would have to handle more producer and consumer traffic, network throughput, and disk I/O.

Even if the cluster is designed to satisfy its ISR and controller quorum requirements, if it's not sized to handle these failures when one region goes down, the surviving regions would be overloaded, resulting in higher latencies, timeouts, and operational instability.

So, for Tier-0 infrastructure, capacity planning should always be done for failure states and not stop at normal operations.

Now let's say Region A comes back online.

The brokers in the recovered region might have to catch up on a significant amount of data before they become fully in sync again.

This can result in a sudden burst of cross-region network traffic and disk I/O across all regions.

Region recovery needs to be controlled so that it doesn't overwhelm the healthy part of the cluster.

This can be achieved by throttling the replica catch-up, with the trade-off of longer recovery time to minimize the impact on live production traffic.

Replication throttling needs a deeper discussion of its own, so we won't go into details here.

The important point is that bringing a failed region back online is not simply a matter of restarting its brokers. We need to make sure that the recovery process itself doesn't impact the business.

Before putting a Tier-0 multi-region Kafka cluster into production, validate these scenarios:

There is no shortage of ways to deploy Kafka across multiple regions. The challenge here is to pick the right architecture that addresses all our durability guarantees.

For Tier-0 workloads, this means designing the architecture around failures first.

What happens when a region goes down? Can we still roll the cluster? What continues to work, and at what point do we deliberately stop accepting writes?

Lastly, can we safely bring back the region that was down without impacting the business?

That is ultimately the difference between simply running Kafka across multiple regions versus actually supporting a mission-critical Tier-0 workload.

#10860. How to Build an Accessible Gold Value Calculator in Vanilla JavaScript

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Ask anyone selling an old chain or a broken ring what they want to know, and it's one thing: what is this worth? A small calculator can answer that in a second, but most of the ones you find online get the details wrong. They ignore troy ounces, treat 14K as 58.5% one day and 58.3% the next, or show a "value" that no buyer will ever pay.

In this tutorial, we'll build a gold value calculator that gets those details right. It runs on plain HTML and JavaScript, updates as the user types, works with a screen reader, keeps its state in a shareable URL and pulls a live spot price through a small Node.js endpoint. The calculation lives in one pure function, so we can test it in a few lines.

Every gold calculator comes down to three inputs: how much the item weighs, how much of that weight is actually gold, and what pure gold is trading for today.

Purity is measured in karats, out of 24. A 14K ring is 14 parts gold and 10 parts other metals, so it's 14 ÷ 24 = 58.3% gold. You'll also see 14K stamped as "585", which is the hallmark many manufacturers use for the same alloy. For a calculator, the karat fraction is the safer default:

The second detail most tutorials miss is the unit. Gold spot prices are quoted in USD per troy ounce, which is 31.1035 grams, not the 28.35-gram ounce on a kitchen scale. Jewelers also use pennyweight (dwt), which is 1.5552 grams. If your calculator multiplies grams by a per-ounce price without converting, every result is wrong by a factor of 31.

Put together, the formula looks like this:

For example, with gold at $2,400 per troy ounce, 10 grams of 14K gold contains 5.83 grams of pure gold, which is worth $449.85 at melt.

That $449.85 is the value of the gold content alone. Refiners and buyers pay a percentage of it, because refining, assaying and handling cost money. Depending on the buyer, the item and the market, the payout can sit well below melt value.

That gap is why good production tools separate the two numbers. A dedicated scrap jewelry gold calculator, like the one The Alloy Market offers, lets sellers estimate what their gold is worth from its weight and karat before they send anything in. Our calculator does the same job with a payout field: leave it at 100% to see melt value, or enter a buyer's rate to see a realistic offer.

Start with real form controls and a visible

A few choices here matter more than they look:

A little CSS gives touch-friendly controls. Keeping inputs at 16px (1rem) also stops iOS Safari from zooming in when a field gets focus.

The biggest problem with most calculator snippets is that the math is buried inside an event handler, mixed with getElementById calls. You can't test it, and you can't reuse it on a server. Put the calculation and validation in their own module instead:

Both functions take plain values and return plain values. They don't know a DOM exists, which means the same file can run in the browser, in Node.js and in your test runner.

Notice that we don't round inside the calculation. Rounding happens once, at display time. Rounding in the middle of a chain of multiplications is a classic way to end up a few cents off.

Now connect the form to the module. Listening for the input event on the form itself catches changes from every field, including the dropdowns, so there's no need to wire up each control separately.

With the markup, CSS and script in place, here's the finished calculator showing our 10 g, 14K example with an 80% buyer payout:

Asking users to type the spot price works, but prefilling it is friendlier. Don't call a metals price API straight from the browser, though. Your API key would be visible to anyone who opens DevTools, and every visitor would spend one of your API calls.

Instead, put a small endpoint in front of your price provider and cache the result. This example uses Express and the fetch built into Node.js 18 and later:

Three things keep this endpoint dependable:

On the page, the loadSpot() function from Step 3 fills the field and shows the time of the price. The field stays editable, so a seller who has a quote from a specific buyer can type that instead.

A seller comparing offers might want to send their numbers to a family member, or come back to them tomorrow. Because every input has a name, turning the form into a query string is one line with URLSearchParams.

The syncUrl() function in Step 3 runs on every change and uses history.replaceState instead of pushState, so typing doesn't fill the back button with dozens of entries. On load, restoreFromUrl() puts the values back. A link like this opens with the form already filled in:

This is also the progressive enhancement story. If JavaScript fails to load, the form still submits as a GET request. Your server can read the same parameters, import the same gold.js and render the result in HTML.

Because goldValue() is a pure function, testing it needs no browser and no mocks. Node.js ships with a built-in test runner, so there's nothing to install:

Run it with node --test. (Add "type": "module" to your package.json so Node treats the files as ES modules.) The troy ounce test is the one worth keeping forever: if anyone "simplifies" the conversion later, it fails immediately.

A quick checklist for putting this calculator on a real store:

A gold calculator looks like a ten-line script, but the details are what make it useful: converting troy ounces properly, separating melt value from payout, keeping the math testable and making the result accessible and shareable. The same pattern of a pure calculation module, a thin DOM layer and a cached server endpoint works for any calculator where the price depends on several inputs, from shipping estimates to currency conversion.

Mathematically, 14K is 14 ÷ 24 = 58.3%. Many manufacturers stamp 14K jewelry "585" and alloy it to that standard, so both numbers are common. Pick one, state it in the dropdown, and use it consistently.

Let the user choose. Most people weigh jewelry in grams, but spot prices are quoted per troy ounce, so the calculator should accept either and convert internally.

Yes. Drop Step 4 and let users type the spot price, or have a scheduled job write the current price into a static JSON file. Everything else runs entirely in the browser.

#10857. Scientists built the hardest AI test ever and the results are surprising

As artificial intelligence systems began scoring extremely high on long used academic benchmarks, researchers noticed a growing issue. The tests that once challenged machines were no longer difficult enough. Well known evaluations such as the Massive Multitask Language Understanding (MMLU) exam, which had previously been seen as demanding, now fail to properly measure the capabilities of today's advanced AI models.

To solve this problem, a worldwide group of nearly 1,000 researchers, including a professor from Texas A&M University, developed a new type of test. Their goal was to build an exam that is broad, difficult, and grounded in expert human knowledge in ways that current AI systems still struggle to handle.

The result is "Humanity's Last Exam" (HLE), a 2,500 question assessment covering mathematics, humanities, natural sciences, ancient languages, and a wide range of highly specialized academic fields. Details of the project appear in a paper published in Nature, and additional information about the exam is available at lastexam.ai.

Among the many contributors is Dr. Tung Nguyen, instructional associate professor in the Department of Computer Science and Engineering at Texas A&M. Nguyen helped write and refine many of the exam questions.

"When AI systems start performing extremely well on human benchmarks, it's tempting to think they're approaching human-level understanding," Nguyen said. "But HLE reminds us that intelligence isn't just about pattern recognition -- it's about depth, context and specialized expertise."

The purpose of the exam was not to trick or defeat human test takers. Instead, the goal was to carefully identify areas where AI systems still fall short.

Specialists from around the world wrote and reviewed the questions included in Humanity's Last Exam. Each problem was carefully designed so it has one clear, verifiable answer. The questions were also crafted to prevent quick solutions through simple internet searches.

The topics come from advanced academic challenges. Some tasks involve translating ancient Palmyrene inscriptions, while others require identifying tiny anatomical structures in birds or analyzing detailed features of Biblical Hebrew pronunciation.

Researchers tested every question against leading AI systems. If any model was able to answer a question correctly, that question was removed from the final exam. This process ensured the test remained just beyond what current AI systems can reliably solve.

Early testing confirmed that the strategy worked. Even powerful AI models struggled with the exam. GPT-4o achieved a score of 2.7 percent, while Claude 3.5 Sonnet reached 4.1 percent. OpenAI's o1 model performed somewhat better with 8 percent. The most capable systems so far, including Gemini 3.1 Pro and Claude Opus 4.6, have reached accuracy levels between about 40 percent and 50 percent.

Nguyen explained that the issue of AI surpassing older tests is more than a technical concern. He contributed 73 of the 2,500 publicly available questions in HLE, the second highest number among contributors, and wrote the most questions related to mathematics and computer science.

"Without accurate assessment tools, policymakers, developers and users risk misinterpreting what AI systems can actually do," he said. "Benchmarks provide the foundation for measuring progress and identifying risks."

According to the research team, high scores on tests originally designed for humans do not necessarily indicate genuine intelligence. Those benchmarks mainly measure how well AI can complete specific tasks created for human learners, rather than capturing deeper understanding.

Despite the dramatic name, Humanity's Last Exam is not meant to suggest that humans are becoming obsolete. Instead, it highlights the large amount of knowledge and expertise that still remains uniquely human.

"This isn't a race against AI," Nguyen said. "It's a method for understanding where these systems are strong and where they struggle. That understanding helps us build safer, more reliable technologies. And, importantly, it reminds us why human expertise still matters."

Humanity's Last Exam is designed to serve as a durable and transparent benchmark for future AI systems. To support that goal, the researchers have released some questions publicly while keeping the majority hidden so that AI models cannot simply memorize the answers.

"For now, Humanity's Last Exam stands as one of the clearest assessments of the gap between AI and human intelligence," Nguyen said, "and despite rapid technological advances, it remains wide."

Nguyen emphasized that the scale of the project demonstrates the value of collaboration across disciplines and countries.

"What made this project extraordinary was the scale," he said. "Experts from nearly every discipline contributed. It wasn't just computer scientists; it was historians, physicists, linguists, medical researchers. That diversity is exactly what exposes the gaps in today's AI systems -- perhaps ironically, it's humans working together."

#10854. Scientists discover AI can make humans more creative

Artificial intelligence (AI) is commonly viewed as a technology designed to automate work and potentially replace human labor. However, new research from Swansea University offers a different perspective. The findings suggest that AI can also function as a creative collaborator that encourages exploration, engagement, and inspiration.

Researchers from the University's Computer Science Department carried out one of the largest studies so far examining how people work alongside AI during creative design tasks. More than 800 participants joined an online experiment where they used an AI-supported system to design virtual cars.

Rather than quietly optimizing designs behind the scenes, the system used a method called MAP-Elites to produce visual galleries filled with many different design possibilities. These galleries showed a wide spectrum of car concepts, including highly effective designs, unusual ideas, and even some intentionally flawed options.

Turing Fellow Dr. Sean Walton, Associate Professor of Computer Science and the study's lead author, explained: "People often think of AI as something that speeds up tasks or improves efficiency, but our findings suggest something far more interesting. When people were shown AI-generated design suggestions, they spent more time on the task, produced better designs and felt more involved. It was not just about efficiency. It was about creativity and collaboration."

Why Traditional AI Evaluation May Be Too Limited

The study, published in the ACM journal Transactions on Interactive Intelligent Systems, also highlights a problem with how AI design tools are typically assessed. Standard metrics often focus on simple behaviors, such as how frequently users click on or copy AI suggestions. According to the researchers, these measures overlook important aspects of the experience, including how the technology influences people's thoughts, emotions, and willingness to explore new ideas.

The Swansea researchers argue that AI systems should be evaluated using broader methods that capture these deeper effects. Understanding how AI shapes human thinking and engagement could provide a more complete picture of its impact.

Dr. Walton emphasized that variety in AI-generated output played a crucial role in the experiment. "Our study highlights the importance of diversity in AI output. Participants responded most positively to galleries that included a wide variety of ideas, including bad ones! These helped them move beyond their initial assumptions and explore a broader design space. This structured diversity prevented early fixation and encouraged creative risk-taking.

"As AI becomes increasingly embedded in creative fields, from engineering and architecture to music and game design, understanding how humans and intelligent systems work together is essential. As the technology evolves, the question is not only what AI can do but how it can help us think, create and collaborate more effectively."

Materials provided by Swansea University. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10852. AI-powered robot learns how to harvest tomatoes more efficiently

Farm labor shortages are pushing agriculture toward greater automation, especially when it comes to harvesting. But not all crops are easy for machines to handle. Tomatoes, for example, grow in clusters, which means a robot must carefully select ripe fruit while leaving unripe ones untouched. This requires precise control and smart decision-making.

To tackle this challenge, Assistant Professor Takuya Fujinaga of Osaka Metropolitan University's Graduate School of Engineering developed a system that trains robots to assess how easy each tomato is to harvest before attempting to pick it.

His approach combines image recognition with statistical analysis to determine the best angle for picking each fruit. The robot analyzes visual details such as the tomato itself, its stems, and whether it is hidden behind leaves or other parts of the plant. These inputs guide the robot in choosing the most effective way to approach and pick the fruit.

From Detection to "Harvest-Ease" Decision-Making

This method shifts away from traditional systems that focus only on detecting and identifying fruit. Instead, Fujinaga introduces what he calls "harvest-ease estimation." "This moves beyond simply asking 'can a robot pick a tomato?' to thinking about 'how likely is a successful pick?', which is more meaningful for real-world farming," he explained.

In testing, the system achieved an 81% success rate, exceeding expectations. About one-quarter of the successful picks came from tomatoes that were harvested from the side after an initial front-facing attempt failed. This indicates the robot can adjust its approach when the first attempt is not successful.

The research underscores how many variables affect robotic harvesting, including how tomatoes cluster, the shape and position of stems, surrounding leaves, and visual obstruction. "This research establishes 'ease of harvesting' as a quantitatively evaluable metric, bringing us one step closer to the realization of agricultural robots that can make informed decisions and act intelligently," Fujinaga said.

Future of Human-Robot Collaboration in Farming

Looking ahead, Fujinaga envisions robots that can independently judge when crops are ready to be picked. "This is expected to usher in a new form of agriculture where robots and humans collaborate," he explained. "Robots will automatically harvest tomatoes that are easy to pick, while humans will handle the more challenging fruits."

The findings were published in Smart Agricultural Technology.

Materials provided by Osaka Metropolitan University. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10851. What Breaks When a Self-Hosted LLM Gateway Hits 1,000 Concurrent Streams

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

We sent 1,000 streaming calls at the same moment through three self-hosted LLM gateways: the Langfuse AI Gateway, the LiteLLM proxy and AcruxCore. Each gateway was limited to 2 CPUs. As shipped, Langfuse finished 261 of the 1,000 streams and LiteLLM finished 403. Neither failure came from the gateway's own code.

Langfuse's failures came from Linux's open-file limit, which Docker sets to 1,024 by default. LiteLLM had that problem too, plus a second one: an empty key cache sent hundreds of database lookups at once, and the ones that waited longer than 10 seconds were rejected. This article shows how each failure looked, how we traced it to its cause, and the settings that fixed it.

This benchmark measured the resource consumption of three LLM gateways under scaling concurrent streaming loads.

Full latency, memory, and feature results are available in the Langfuse vs LiteLLM vs AcruxCore benchmark.

First-token times are medians of the streams that succeeded. Up to 250 streams, every gateway succeeded. Then the errors started, and they did not look alike:

A 502 and a 503 point at different layers, so we expected two different causes. Two of the three turned out to share one cause.

Linux counts every network connection as an open file, and it caps how many open files one process may hold. The cap has two values. The soft limit is the cap that is in force now. The hard limit is the ceiling: a process may raise its own soft limit up to that value, and no further.

A streaming gateway holds two connections for each stream: one from the client, and one to the model provider. That makes about 1,000 open files at 500 streams, and about 2,000 at 1,000 streams. A gateway also needs a few more files for its database, its logs and its own libraries.

Docker on our machine gave every container a soft limit of 1,024 and a hard limit of 524,288. All three gateways received the same two numbers. What differed was how each program treats them when it starts:

Node.js raises its own soft limit to the hard limit at startup. The Rust and Python services keep 1,024, and neither project's Compose file sets a higher value. So the two gateways ran out of open files near 500 streams, which is the point where the errors started.

The out-of-files error appeared as a failed connection, not as a clear message. The Langfuse gateway's logs showed failed connections to OpenAI, and also failed connections to Langfuse's own web service, which the gateway asks to check each key. Both come from one cause: a process that cannot open another file cannot open another socket. Its client then receives a 502.

Read the limit the process actually has, not the one you think Docker gave it. Process 1 is usually the main process in a container:

If the gateway runs under a supervisor or several workers, check each worker's process ID instead of process 1. To see how close a process is to its limit under load, count its open file descriptors:

Raise the limit for the gateway service in its Compose file. We used 65,536 for all three gateways:

The same ulimits block works on the LiteLLM service. On Kubernetes or another platform, check /proc//limits inside a running container rather than assuming a value.

With the open-file limit raised, the Langfuse gateway finished all 1,000 streams. LiteLLM still returned 503 errors. To separate the two causes, we ran LiteLLM with one worker and only the open-file limit raised. All 500 streams succeeded in that ladder, but 595 of 1,000 failed with 503.

The 503 message names the authentication database, so we read LiteLLM's logs and its source. The log gave the same reason for all 840 failed streams in these runs, 595 in that ladder and 245 in the cold-cache runs below:

When 500 calls arrive while the key is not in the cache, each call looks the key up in Postgres. All of those lookups queue for 10 connections. The worker was also using its whole CPU allowance at this point, which slows every query it sends and receives. We infer that this CPU load lengthens the queue, but we did not measure it separately. Whatever the share of each, the lookups at the back of the queue miss the 10-second deadline.

If that explanation is right, the failures should depend on whether the key is already in the cache. So we ran 500 streams four more times. Three runs started after 75 seconds with no calls, which is longer than the 60-second cache. One run started right after a single warm-up call:

The warm run had no failures. The cold runs failed by different amounts, 161, 84 and 0, because the result depends on how many lookups miss the deadline, and that changes from run to run. This probably explains why the earlier ladder succeeded at 500 and failed at 1,000. A run is a race between the lookup queue and the deadline, so a single ladder does not show where the limit is.

The warm-up result is the one we measured. Send one call with each virtual key after LiteLLM starts, and avoid quiet periods longer than 60 seconds before a traffic spike. A steady flow of traffic keeps the key in the cache.

LiteLLM also exposes three settings that act on this path. We did not test them, so treat them as places to look rather than proven fixes:

We raised the open-file limit to 65,536 for all three gateways and gave LiteLLM 2 workers, one per CPU, as its production guide recommends for a single machine. Then we ran the full ladder again:

The open-file errors were gone for every gateway, and the Langfuse gateway gave the fastest first token of the three. LiteLLM still returned 120 × 503 at 500. That level started about 60 seconds after the first call of the ladder, so the cached key had probably just expired. All 1,000 succeeded at the next level, which started within 60 seconds of the 500 level.

Once nothing failed, CPU decided the first-token times. LiteLLM used its whole 2-CPU allowance from 100 streams on, and its median first token took 19.2 seconds at 1,000 streams. AcruxCore also used both CPUs from 100 streams on, and its first token at 1,000 streams was 3.8 s at the median and 9.7 s at p95. The Langfuse gateway peaked at 1.65 CPUs and 168 MiB, with a first token of 1.7 s at the median and 9.6 s at p95.

Each gateway also needs the rest of its stack, and that stack uses CPU too. While the Langfuse gateway served 500 streams, its whole stack used up to 3.1 CPUs, because the web service checked keys and the worker and ClickHouse stored usage. AcruxCore's whole stack peaked at 4.0 CPUs, because its API writes a trace for every call to Postgres. Size the stack around the gateway, not only the gateway container.

Talha Anwar is a senior AI and full-stack engineer and writes about building and running AI systems at talhaanwar.com.

#10849. DNA robots could deliver drugs and hunt viruses inside your body

Imagine tiny robots made from DNA moving through the bloodstream, delivering drugs exactly where they are needed and targeting threats such as cancer cells or viruses. These microscopic machines could also assemble ultra-precise data storage systems and computing devices at the nanometer scale. Although the possibilities are remarkable, most DNA robots today remain in early experimental stages and are better understood as proof of concept rather than practical tools.

Researchers are exploring how DNA can be engineered into working machines using creative design approaches. These include building rigid DNA joints, incorporating flexible components, and using folding techniques inspired by origami. By applying principles from larger-scale robotics such as rigid, compliant and origami robots, scientists are adapting familiar mechanical concepts to the nanoscale. This allows DNA-based systems to carry out controlled and repeatable tasks despite their extremely small size.

Guiding the motion of DNA robots in a constantly shifting molecular environment is a major challenge. To address this, scientists have developed control systems that help these machines behave in predictable ways. One important method involves DNA strand displacement, a biochemical process that enables precise programming of movement using specific DNA sequences labeled as "fuel" and "structure."

In addition to biochemical control, external physical signals such as electric fields, magnetic fields, and light can direct how these robots move. Together, these approaches provide a toolkit for fine-tuning the behavior of DNA machines with a high degree of accuracy.

The potential uses for DNA robots extend well beyond laboratory experiments. In medicine, they could function as "nano-surgeons," locating diseased cells and delivering targeted treatments with precision. Researchers are also exploring whether these machines could capture viruses like SARS-CoV-2, with future systems potentially operating as fully autonomous drug delivery platforms.

DNA robots may also play a role in advanced manufacturing. Acting as programmable templates, they could position nanoparticles with sub-nanometer accuracy. This capability could lead to breakthroughs in molecular computing and highly efficient optical devices that outperform current technologies.

Despite rapid progress, several obstacles remain. Moving from large-scale systems to molecular machines introduces challenges such as Brownian motion, which makes precise control more difficult. Many current DNA robot designs are still relatively simple and operate in isolation, limiting their usefulness in complex real-world environments.

There are also gaps in foundational knowledge. Researchers still lack detailed databases describing the mechanical properties of DNA structures, and simulation tools for predicting behavior at this scale are not yet fully developed.

To overcome these barriers, scientists emphasize the need for collaboration across disciplines. Proposed solutions include creating standardized DNA "parts libraries," using artificial intelligence to improve design and simulation, and advancing bio-manufacturing methods. Progress in these areas will be essential for scaling DNA robots and integrating them into practical applications in healthcare, manufacturing, and beyond.

"The robots of tomorrow won't just be made of metal and plastic," says the research team. "They will be biological, programmable, and intelligent. They will be the tools that allow us to finally master the molecular world."

#10847. AI breakthrough cuts energy use by 100x while boosting accuracy

Artificial intelligence is consuming enormous amounts of electricity in the United States. According to the International Energy Agency, AI systems and data centers used about 415 terawatt hours of power in 2024. That accounts for more than 10% of the country's total electricity production, and demand is projected to double by 2030.

This rapid growth has raised concerns about sustainability. In response, researchers at a School of Engineering have created a proof-of-concept AI system designed to be far more efficient. Their approach could reduce energy use by up to 100 times while also improving performance on tasks.

A Hybrid Approach Called Neuro-Symbolic AI

The research comes from the laboratory of Matthias Scheutz, Karol Family Applied Technology Professor. His team is developing neuro-symbolic AI, which combines traditional neural networks with symbolic reasoning. This method mirrors how people approach problems by breaking them into steps and categories.

The work will be presented at the International Conference of Robotics and Automation in Vienna in May and will appear in the conference proceedings.

Teaching Robots to See, Understand, and Act

Unlike familiar large language models (LLMs) such as ChatGPT and Gemini, the team focuses on AI systems used in robotics. These systems are known as visual-language-action (VLA) models. They extend LLM capabilities by incorporating vision and physical movement.

VLA models take in visual data from cameras and instructions from language, then translate that information into real-world actions. For example, they can control a robot's wheels, arms, or fingers to complete a task.

Why Traditional AI Struggles With Simple Tasks

Conventional VLA systems rely heavily on data and trial-and-error learning. If a robot is asked to stack blocks into a tower, it must first analyze the scene, identify each block, and determine how to place them correctly.

This process often leads to mistakes. Shadows may confuse the system about a block's shape, or the robot may place pieces incorrectly, causing the structure to collapse.

These errors are similar to the problems seen in LLMs. Just as robots can misplace blocks, chatbots can generate false or misleading outputs. Examples include fabricating legal cases or producing images with unrealistic details such as extra fingers.

How Symbolic Reasoning Improves Accuracy and Efficiency

Symbolic reasoning offers a different strategy. Instead of relying only on patterns from data, it uses rules and abstract concepts such as shape and balance. This allows the system to plan more effectively and avoid unnecessary trial and error.

"Like an LLM, VLA models act on statistical results from large training sets of similar scenarios, but that can lead to errors," said Scheutz. "A neuro-symbolic VLA can apply rules that limit the amount of trial and error during learning and get to a solution much faster. Not only does it complete the task much faster, but the time spent on training the system is significantly reduced."

The researchers tested their system using the Tower of Hanoi puzzle, a classic problem that requires careful planning.

The neuro-symbolic VLA achieved a 95% success rate, compared with just 34% for standard systems. When given a more complex version of the puzzle that it had not encountered before, the hybrid system still succeeded 78% of the time. Traditional models failed every attempt.

Training time also dropped sharply. The new system learned the task in only 34 minutes, while conventional models required more than a day and a half.

Massive Energy Savings in Training and Use

Energy consumption was reduced dramatically as well. Training the neuro-symbolic model required only 1% of the energy used by a standard VLA system. During operation, it used just 5% of the energy needed by conventional approaches.

Scheutz compared this inefficiency to everyday AI tools. "These systems are just trying to predict the next word or action in a sequence, but that can be imperfect, and they can come up with inaccurate results or hallucinations. Their energy expense is often disproportionate to the task. For example, when you search on Google, the AI summary at the top of the page consumes up to 100 times more energy than the generation of the website listings."

The Growing Strain of AI on Power Infrastructure

As AI adoption accelerates across industries, demand for computing power continues to climb. Companies are building increasingly large data centers, some of which require hundreds of megawatts of electricity. That level of consumption can exceed the needs of entire small cities.

This trend has sparked a race to expand infrastructure, raising concerns about long-term energy limits.

The researchers suggest that current approaches based on LLMs and VLAs may not be sustainable in the long run. While these systems are powerful, they consume large amounts of energy and can still produce unreliable results.

In contrast, neuro-symbolic AI offers a different direction. By combining learning with structured reasoning, it may provide a more efficient and dependable foundation for future AI systems.

#10843. This new chip survives 1300°F (700°C) and could change AI forever

Modern electronics power everything from smartphones to satellites, but they all share a major limitation. Heat. Once temperatures climb above roughly 200 degrees Celsius, most devices begin to break down. For decades, this thermal barrier has been one of the toughest challenges in engineering.

Researchers at the University of Southern California now believe they have found a way past that limit.

In a study published on March 26, 2026 in Science, a team led by Joshua Yang, Arthur B. Freeman Chair Professor at the Ming Hsieh Department of Electrical and Computer Engineering at the USC Viterbi School of Engineering and the USC School of Advanced Computing, unveiled a new type of memory device that continues to operate at 700 degrees Celsius (~1300 degrees Fahrenheit). That temperature exceeds molten lava and goes far beyond anything previously achieved for this class of technology. The device showed no sign of failure. In fact, 700 degrees was simply the maximum their equipment could test.

"You may call it a revolution," Yang said. "It is the best high-temperature memory ever demonstrated."

The new device is known as a memristor, a nanoscale component that can both store data and perform computations. It is constructed like a microscopic layered structure, with two electrodes on either side and a thin ceramic layer in between.

Jian Zhao, the study's first author, built the device using tungsten for the top electrode, hafnium oxide ceramic in the middle, and graphene for the bottom layer. Tungsten has the highest melting point of any element, while graphene, a single-atom-thick sheet of carbon, is known for its exceptional strength and heat resistance.

This combination produced remarkable performance. The device retained data for more than 50 hours at 700 degrees without needing to be refreshed. It also endured over one billion switching cycles at that temperature and operated at just 1.5 volts with speeds measured in tens of nanoseconds.

The discovery was not part of the team's original plan. They were initially attempting to create a different graphene-based device, which did not work as intended. Along the way, they encountered something surprising.

"To be honest, it was by accident, as most discoveries are," Yang said. "If you can predict it, it's usually not surprising, and probably not significant enough."

Further investigation revealed why the device performed so well. In conventional electronics, heat causes metal atoms in the top electrode to slowly migrate through the ceramic layer. Eventually, they reach the bottom electrode, creating a permanent connection that short-circuits the device and leaves it stuck in the on state.

Graphene prevents this failure. Its interaction with tungsten is, as Yang described it, similar to oil and water. Tungsten atoms that approach the graphene surface cannot attach to it. Without a stable point to settle, they drift away instead of forming a conductive bridge. This prevents short circuits and preserves the device's function even under extreme heat.

The researchers confirmed this mechanism using advanced electron microscopy, spectroscopy, and quantum-level simulations. By understanding what happens at the atomic interface, they have turned an unexpected result into a principle that could guide future designs. Other materials with similar surface properties could be identified, which may help scale the technology for industrial production.

Electronics capable of operating above 500 degrees Celsius have long been a goal for space exploration. Venus, for example, has a surface temperature around that level, and every lander sent there has failed in part due to extreme heat. Current silicon-based chips cannot survive such conditions.

"We are now above 700 degrees, and we suspect it will go higher," Yang said.

The potential applications go far beyond space missions. Geothermal energy systems require electronics that can function deep underground, where surrounding rock can glow red-hot. Nuclear and fusion systems also expose equipment to intense heat. Even in everyday settings, durability improves significantly. A device rated for 700 degrees would be extremely robust at the roughly 125-degree temperatures often reached inside automotive electronics.

Why It Matters for Artificial Intelligence

In addition to storing data, the device offers a major advantage for artificial intelligence. Many AI systems rely heavily on matrix multiplication, a mathematical operation used in tasks like image recognition and language processing. Traditional computers perform these calculations step by step, consuming large amounts of energy.

Memristors approach the problem differently. By using Ohm's Law, where voltage times conductance equals current, the device performs calculations directly as electricity flows through it. The result is obtained instantly as the measured current.

"Over 92 percent of the computing in AI systems like ChatGPT is nothing but matrix multiplication," Yang said. "This type of device can perform that in the most efficient way, orders of magnitude faster and at lower energy."

Yang and three co-authors of the study (Qiangfei Xia, Miao Hu, and Ning Ge) have already co-founded a company called TetraMem to commercialize memristor-based AI chips at room temperature. Their lab is already using working chips from TetraMem for machine learning tasks. The high-temperature version described in this research could extend those capabilities to environments where traditional electronics cannot operate, allowing devices such as spacecraft or industrial sensors to process data directly on site.

From Lab Prototype to Real-World Technology

Despite the promising results, Yang emphasizes that practical applications are still some distance away. Memory is only one part of a complete computing system. High-temperature logic circuits will also need to be developed and integrated. In addition, the current devices were built manually at very small scales in a laboratory setting, so manufacturing at scale will take time.

"This is the first step," Yang said. "It's still a long way to go. But logically, you can see: now it makes it possible. The missing component has been made."

From a manufacturing perspective, two of the materials used in the device, tungsten and hafnium oxide, are already widely used in semiconductor production. Graphene is newer but is actively being developed by major companies such as TSMC and Samsung, and it has already been produced at wafer scale in research environments.

The work was conducted through the CONCRETE Center, short for Center of Neuromorphic Computing under Extreme Environments, a multi-university Center of Excellence led by USC and supported by the Air Force Office of Scientific Research and the Air Force Research Laboratory. Key experimental work was carried out in collaboration with Dr. Sabyasachi Ganguli's team at the AFRL Materials Lab in Dayton, Ohio, while theoretical analysis involved USC researchers and collaborators at Kumamoto University in Japan.

For Yang, publication in Science reflects more than a single achievement.

"Space exploration has never been so real, so close, and at such a large scale," he said. "This paper represents a critical leap into a much larger, more exciting frontier."

Materials provided by University of Southern California. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10842. How to Use Developer Time Tracking Data to Improve Sprint Planning and Estimates

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Sprint estimates usually fail because development teams heavily rely on memory and assumptions instead of evidence. They assume similar tasks will take the same amount of time. Based on these half-baked assumptions, most managers plan around ideal sprint working hours. The backlog then fills with commitments that look realistic on paper but often end up delayed.

Actual development rarely follows that plan. There are always unexpected bugs and task dependencies that consume more time than teams account for in their estimates. Even the buffer time set aside for unexpected work may not be enough. This causes tasks to take longer than expected and pushes sprint commitments behind schedule.

Developer time tracking helps teams make better estimates with historical data. It shows how long previous tasks actually took, which provides managers with an evidence-based reference when they are planning similar work.

Reviewing this data before committing to a sprint scope turns estimation from guesswork into an evidence-based process.

Historical time data offers deeper insights, depending on how you collect and analyze it. Most importantly, it explains why planned work took longer than expected.

Before sprint planning, the following records should be reviewed.

Completed task duration: Check the time spent on finished tasks. Managers should compare similar work to understand typical development effort.

Actual versus estimated hours: Management should observe and identify differences between planned and recorded hours. If there are repeated delays, to some extent, it indicates inaccurate estimates.

Carryover work: Review unfinished tasks from previous sprints. Determine whether poor estimates or external delays caused the spillover..

Interruptions: Measure time spent on urgent requests and production issues. These activities reduce the development capacity that you had planned earlier.

Meetings and ceremonies: Review the time being spent on planning sessions and sprint reviews.

Bug-fix time: Examine hours spent resolving defects. You can also compare it to the time for developing new features.

Blocked tasks: Managers should account for the tasks delayed by missing approvals or technical dependencies.

Capacity differences: Compare team availability across sprints. Include leave and staffing changes in that time as well.

These records provide the most value when reviewed together. For example, a task may finish three days late even if the development work stayed within its estimated hours. The actual issue could be an external dependency.

Teams should understand these differences before using historical data to estimate future work.

Historical records provide a useful starting point for estimating new tasks. However, teams must interpret the data rather than simply copy previous estimates.

The following approaches can improve estimation accuracy.

The first step is to consider completed tasks that resemble upcoming work. Managers should compare their requirements and technical complexity. Moreover, the development environment should be considered.

For example, suppose a team needs to estimate a new API integration. Three previous integrations required 12, 15, and 18 hours of active work.

The average is 15 hours. This gives developers a reference point for the new estimate.

However, the average doesn't tell the entire story. The new integration may involve unfamiliar authentication methods or more complicated testing.

Review the reasons behind differences in previous task durations. Check whether developers encountered unexpected bugs or unclear requirements.

As a project manager, I'd consider these details before estimating for the next project. In my experience, they provide more context than an average alone.

Inaccurate estimates can happen; it doesn’t mean that there is a definite planning problem. However, repeated overruns across similar tasks may reveal an underlying issue.

Research suggests the pattern is widespread. A review of software estimation surveys reports that 60–80% of software projects encounter effort overruns, with the average overrun falling between 30% and 40%.

Start by calculating the difference between estimated and actual effort. Here is the formula for it.

Estimation variance (%) = [(Actual hours − Estimated hours) ÷ Estimated hours] × 100

Suppose a task was estimated at 10 hours but needed 14 hours. The actual effort exceeded the estimate by 40%.

You can compare the results across similar tasks.

For example, database migrations may repeatedly require more time than expected. This means that the team might be overlooking data validation or additional testing. Once the cause is clear, adjust future estimates accordingly.

Avoid increasing every estimate by the same percentage. Different types of work involve different risks. Teams can also break large tasks into smaller parts. This makes hidden development activities easier to identify.

The aim is to correct recurring estimation errors without unnecessarily increasing the entire sprint backlog.

Task duration doesn't always reflect the hours spent developing a feature.

A study of developer workdays by Meyer and colleagues, which tracked 2,197 hours of activity across 20 developers, found that coding accounted for around 21% of the typical day. Collaborative work such as meetings and email took up roughly 24%.

Consider a developer who starts a feature on Monday and finishes it on Wednesday. The task took three days to complete. This is pretty straightforward, and the estimation could be based on it; however, the developer may have spent several hours in meetings. Other than that, urgent support requests could also have consumed additional time.

The actual development effort could be much lower than three full working days. This distinction matters during estimation.

Teams should record focused development time separately from interruptions. They should also track waiting periods caused by blocked tasks. A more appropriate approach would be to use active development hours to estimate similar future work. They should also account for recurring interruptions when calculating sprint capacity.

For example, a task may regularly wait for security approval. Adding more development hours to its estimate won't resolve that delay.

Instead, the team should recognize the dependency and consider its effect on delivery.

If managers are able to separate these activities, it will help create better estimates without confusing effort with elapsed time.

A precise estimate can create false confidence when a task involves substantial uncertainty.

Suppose similar tasks previously required between 12 and 20 hours. If you estimate the next task at exactly 14 hours, it will overlook important differences.

A range of 12–20 hours provides a clearer picture of the possible effort. Developers can then discuss what might push the task toward either end of the range.

For example, the lower estimate can be directly applied to integration, and the upper estimate might be considered for unfamiliar code or additional testing.

In such scenarios, if the requirements stay unclear, consider a short technical investigation before finalizing the estimate. This can help identify dependencies and reduce uncertainty.

However, estimate ranges should still support practical planning decisions. Teams need to determine how much capacity to reserve for the work.

Choose a planning assumption that reflects the task's risk and available capacity.

As developers learn more about the task, they can refine the estimate.

Historical time data should affect how much work a team accepts during sprint planning. Better estimates offer little value if teams continue making unrealistic commitments.

If several sprints end with unfinished tasks, it is best that you first review why the team missed its commitments in the first place. Once you have identified the causes, use those findings to reduce the next sprint's scope.

Teams should prioritize work that supports the sprint goal. Moreover, avoid adding extra tasks just because they appear manageable during planning.

Production incidents and urgent requests can disrupt planned development. To get more reliable estimations, review how much time these activities consumed in previous sprints. Then reserve an appropriate amount of capacity.

Historical estimates become less reliable in case unfamiliar technology or additional dependencies are involved. It is best to break complex tasks into smaller components where possible. This helps developers assess the work more accurately.

Nominal capacity represents scheduled working hours, whereas actual capacity reflects the time available after accounting for other commitments.

The Scrum Guide identifies previous performance and upcoming capacity as important inputs to sprint forecasting.

When you are selecting backlog items, consider both. Additionally, avoid committing every available hour or deducting the same interruption twice.

Historical time data is useful for sprint planning. Most development teams already use project management tools that include basic time tracking features.

However, these tools don't always break down how developers spend their working hours. For clearer time data, a feature-specific time tracker is more useful because it provides more details about tasks and workloads.

TimeBee is one such developer time tracking tool I've used. It tracks working hours and time spent on projects and tasks. It also provides attendance records and workforce activity reports.

With this data, teams can review how much time previous tasks required. For example, developers can record hours spent building a feature. They can also track debugging and revisions separately. Later, managers can compare these records with the original estimates. This helps them plan similar work more accurately.

TimeBee also tracks website and application activity. These reports can help teams understand how working hours are distributed.

However, not all activities reflect actual development effort. Sometimes a developer just spends time reviewing documentation or discussing technical problems. Such work may not always appear in TimeBee as active computer use.

Apart from that, attendance, leave, and overtime records from TimeBee can help managers calculate sprint capacity. They can review developer availability before deciding how much work to include in the next sprint.

One thing to remember is that developer time tracking should help teams understand workload patterns. It shouldn't be used to monitor every minute of a developer's day. Management should be careful that the aim is to improve estimates and make more realistic sprint commitments.

Sprint retrospectives should include a review of how the planning is going. If you have made a plan, it doesn’t mean you cannot make changes to it. During sprints, keep comparing original estimates with actual task hours. Track your unfinished work for recurring delays.

Ask developers what caused significant differences. Some tasks may have required additional testing. Others may have been delayed by dependencies. Select one specific improvement for the next sprint.

Not every estimation difference indicates a planning failure. Unexpected technical challenges are part of software development.

The objective is to improve forecasts over time rather than eliminate every variance.

Historical time data provides a more stable foundation for sprint planning. Developer time tracking helps teams understand what is actually happening within the sprint rather than creating unnecessary pressure for them.

Other than that, the data also reveals how interruptions affect available development hours.

However, time data should guide planning rather than dictate it.

1. What is the difference between sprint velocity and time-based capacity planning?

Sprint velocity measures how much work a team has completed during a sprint. It is usually measured in story points. Contrary to this, time-based capacity planning calculates the hours available for planned work. It accounts for meetings and other responsibilities.

Velocity helps predict future delivery based on past performance. Capacity planning shows whether the team has enough time to meet its commitments.

2. Which time tracking metrics are most useful for improving Agile sprint forecasts? The most useful metrics include estimated versus actual hours, task completion time, and unplanned work.

Bug-fix time, rework, and blocked tasks also provide valuable insights. Teams should also review available capacity across previous sprints.

These metrics help sort out recurring delays and estimation errors. They allow teams to forecast workloads more accurately and make realistic sprint commitments.

**3. How can development teams improve estimates when historical time tracking data is incomplete? **

Teams can start with available task records and completed sprint backlogs. Developer feedback can help fill information gaps. Similar tasks from previous projects may also provide useful estimates.

When data is limited, teams should use estimate ranges instead of fixed hours. Consistent time tracking in future sprints will gradually provide more reliable planning data.

4. How can time tracking data help balance workloads across developers during sprint planning?

Time tracking data shows how much time developers spend on assigned tasks and other responsibilities. Managers can review these records to identify uneven workloads. They can then distribute upcoming tasks based on available capacity.

Technical expertise and task complexity should also influence assignments. This helps prevent over commitment and supports a more balanced workload across the development team.
